<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract.

The Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) is a critical tool for securing the software supply chain (SSC), but its practical utility is undermined by inaccuracies in both its generation and its application in vulnerability scanning. This paper presents a large-scale empirical study on 2,414 open-source repositories to address these issues from a practical standpoint. First, we demonstrate that using lock files with strong package managers enables the generation of accurate and consistent SBOMs, establishing a reliable foundation for security analysis. Using this high-fidelity foundation, however, we expose a more fundamental flaw in practice: downstream vulnerability scanners produce a staggering 92.0% false positive rate in our case study. We pinpoint the primary cause as the flagging of vulnerabilities within unreachable code. We then demonstrate that function call analysis can effectively prune 61.9% of these false alarms. Our work validates a practical, two-stage approach for SSC security: first, generate an accurate SBOM using lock files and strong package managers, and second, enrich it with function call analysis to produce actionable, low-noise vulnerability reports that alleviate developers’ alert fatigue.

</div>

<div class="ltx_keywords">

###### Keywords: 

Software Bills of Material; Software Supply Chain Security; Software Package Managers

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 1. </span>Summary of related works on SBOM tools and SBOM-based vulnerability scanners with their associated languages/platforms.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1. </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Software often relies on third-party libraries as dependencies (<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref">Mockus, 2007</a>; <a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">Ellison et al., 2010</a>). These libraries, in turn, have their own dependencies, referred to as transitive dependencies. These hierarchical dependencies form a software supply chain (SSC) (<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref">Ellison et al., 2010</a>). Due to the transitive and often opaque nature of third-party dependency management, SSC-based attacks pose significant challenges to software security (<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref">Alkhadra et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref">Lakshmanan, 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref">Lins et al., 2024</a>). To mitigate such attacks, the United States National Telecommunications and Information Administration (NTIA) proposed the utilization of a software bill of materials (SBOM) (<a href="#bib.bib22" class="ltx_ref">Beg, 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">National Telecommunications and Information Administration, 2021</a>). An SBOM records all direct and transitive dependencies required during the software build process to trace the SSC. In practice, software developers leverage SBOMs to identify potential security risks, thereby enhancing the security of the SSC (<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Benedetti et al., 2024</a>). SBOMs provide insights into the software dependencies. Therefore, it is critical to ensure their accuracy and quality for subsequent analyses.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Recent studies have highlighted the limitations of existing SBOM generators in producing accurate and reliable SBOMs (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Rabbi et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2024</a>). For instance, <a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al. (2024)</a> pointed out that, given the same input files, different SBOM generators often produce SBOMs with inconsistent and inaccurate results. Similarly, <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al. (2023)</a> revealed that discrepancies in SBOM outputs from various generators significantly impact the effectiveness of downstream vulnerability analyses. Moreover, <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">Balliu et al. (2023)</a> demonstrated that even two official SBOM generators provided by CycloneDX (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">OWASP Foundation, 2024</a>), the founder of a popular SBOM standard with the same name, produce different results with considerable variations.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Despite the industrial consensus on using lock files for reproducibility (<a href="#bib.bib53" class="ltx_ref">GitLab Inc., 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref">Snyk, 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib54" class="ltx_ref">Ostorlab, 2025</a>), a concerning methodological disconnect persists in the academic community. As shown in Table <a href="#S0.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 1 ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, numerous recent studies (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Rabbi et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2024</a>) continue to rely on project manifest files as the primary input for vulnerability analysis, inevitably yielding erroneous outcomes. <a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al. (2024)</a> critically exposed the inconsistencies inherent in SBOM generated from project files, which developers use to describe direct dependencies, and advocated for the adoption of lock files. Unfortunately, their claim lacked large-scale empirical experimental validation. In this work, we close this gap by rigorously validating their proposition. We show that inconsistencies observed in prior work are not inherent to the SBOM tools themselves but, rather, are due to the ambiguous nature of the input provided to them. We demonstrate that adhering to lock files and strong package managers (PMs), which are capable of managing direct and transitive dependencies (further details on PMs and their categorization are provided in Section <a href="#S2.SS2" class="ltx_ref" title="2.2. Package Managers (PMs) ‣ 2. Background ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.2</span></a>), is not merely a technical preference but a prerequisite for correctness. Consequently, we argue that future research must standardize on lock files as the definitive input for SBOM generation to ensure accuracy and completeness. We hope that our work will close this debate once and for all.

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

By establishing a method to generate accurate SBOMs, we create a reliable baseline to empirically investigate and quantify a more profound flaw in the downstream vulnerability pipeline. Existing SBOM-based vulnerability scanners often suffer from a high rate of false positives (<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cofano et al., 2024</a>). These tools operate by matching the versions of third-party components listed in an SBOM against a vulnerability database, which maintains records of known vulnerabilities and their affected version ranges. The primary source of these inaccuracies lies in this version-only matching approach. Although a component’s version may be listed as vulnerable, the specific code containing the vulnerability might not be invoked by the application. Consequently, developers are presented with reports containing numrous irrelevant alerts, which creates a significant verification burden and leads to alerts fatigue, a well-documented phenomenon where users become desensitized and start to ignore frequent or repetitive warnings (<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref">Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality, 2019</a>), increasing the risk that genuine, critical vulnerabilities are overlooked.

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

We conduct a large-scale experiment on 2,414 open-source repositories across four programming languages to address these challenges systematically. First, we establish that using lock files as input enables SBOM generators to produce consistent and accurate results, creating a high-fidelity foundation for analysis. Second, using this foundation, we quantify the end-to-end performance of the SBOM-based vulnerability management pipeline, revealing a staggering false positive rate of 92.0% in the manually validated dataset and pinpointing unreachable code as the primary cause. Finally, we validate that function call analysis can effectively prune a significant number of these false positives by 61.9%, paving the way for a more practical vulnerability management strategy that significantly reduces false positives and alleviates developers’ alert fatigue.

</div>

<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para">

Our contributions are summarized as follows:

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We propose to use the lock file instead of the previously used project file as input to SBOM generators, and establish the importance of doing so in order to produce accurate and complete SBOMs. Our large-scale experiments demonstrate that SBOM generators can generate accurate and consistent SBOMs with lock files.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Using these accurate SBOMs, we conduct a large-scale analysis of downstream vulnerability reports. We quantify the significant false positive rates of existing scanners and pinpoint unreachable code as a primary cause of these false alarms.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We propose a best-practice pipeline for generating more reliable vulnerability reports. We validate this approach through function call analysis, demonstrating its effectiveness in significantly reducing false positives and guiding developers toward more effective security practices. We open-source our code on GitHub<span id="footnote1" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span> https://github.com/damaoooo/validation</span></span></span><span id="footnote2" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>2</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">2</span> https://github.com/damaoooo/SBOMVerifierGoPoC</span></span></span> with the corresponding SBOM and vulnerability verification artifacts.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para">

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows: Section <a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref" title="2. Background ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> provides background for this study. We detail our motivation for this research in Section <a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref" title="3. Motivation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>. Section <a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref" title="4. Methodology ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> details our methodology and evaluation setup. Our experimental results are demonstrated in Section <a href="#S5" class="ltx_ref" title="5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>. In Section <a href="#S6" class="ltx_ref" title="6. Discussion ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>, we examine the reasons behind the inconsistencies in SBOM generator outputs and propose best practices for enhancing SSC security. We conclude our paper and explore future directions in Section <a href="#S7" class="ltx_ref" title="7. Conclusions and Future Work ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2. </span>Background

<div id="S2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.1. </span>Software Bill of Materials (SBOM)

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">2.1.1. </span>SBOM Definition

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The NTIA defines an SBOM as a formal record documenting the components and their supply chain relationships used in software development (<a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref">National Telecommunications and Information Administration, 2021</a>). It is a detailed inventory of the software components used in a project. It enables effective tracking and management of dependencies, helping to identify vulnerabilities and mitigate security risks. Typically, it includes information not only about component <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">names</span> and <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">versions</span> but also additional information, like <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">manufacturer</span>, <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">description</span>, <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">licenses</span>, <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">package website</span>, <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">checksum</span>, and other metadata (<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref">Muirí, 2019</a>). Accurately identifying software components is vital for retrieving additional related information. Therefore, in this study, as a foundational step, we focus on verifying the correctness of the <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">name</span> and <span id="S2.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">version</span> of each component, aligning with previous research (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">Balliu et al., 2023</a>). There are several established SBOM formats and standards (<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">Software Package Data Exchange, 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">OWASP Foundation, 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref">Waltermire et al., 2016</a>). Among these, CycloneDX (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">OWASP Foundation, 2024</a>) and SPDX (<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">Software Package Data Exchange, 2024</a>) are two of the most widely adopted formats, offering detailed package information (<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref">Sehgal and Ambili, 2023</a>). Hence, in this study, we select CycloneDX and SPDX for our evaluation (<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref">OWASP Foundation, 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref">Software Package Data Exchange, 2024</a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">2.1.2. </span>SBOM Applications

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

By listing the necessary software components, in theory, SBOMs should enable users to identify vulnerabilities and assess the security posture of software binaries without having access to their source code (<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Benedetti et al., 2024</a>). Through a detailed inventory of software components and their versions, the authors in (<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>) proposed referencing this information with vulnerability databases to detect vulnerable components and evaluate potential risks. Furthermore, SBOMs facilitate license management by providing information about the licenses associated with each software component. This ensures compliance with legal requirements and helps organizations identify potential licensing conflicts (<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref">Dalia et al., 2024</a>). Unfortunately, the poor quality of the SBOM tools output renders these tasks practically impossible, as explained in the previously mentioned references (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">Balliu et al., 2023</a>).

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.2. </span>Package Managers (PMs)

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

PMs are widely used in software engineering to manage third-party dependencies (<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref">Muhammad et al., 2019</a>). PMs typically use a so-called “project file” that lists the required third-party libraries, to handle transitive dependencies automatically, to verify whether these dependencies are available, and to install the third-party packages based on the resolved dependencies (<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref">Spinellis, 2012</a>). Nowadays, mainstream programming languages are equipped with their own PMs (<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref">Decan et al., 2019</a>).

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

It is important to note that third-party libraries often have their own dependencies. Depending on how these transitive dependencies are managed, we categorize PMs into <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">strong PMs</span> and <span id="S2.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">weak PMs</span>. Strong PMs explicitly resolve all dependencies, both direct and transitive, and generate a lock file that records the exact versions of each dependency used. In contrast, weak PMs do not explicitly resolve transitive dependencies in a lock file; instead, they only retrieve transitive dependencies during the package installation process. The work in (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>) highlighted that missing transitive dependencies present a significant challenge for SBOM tools in generating accurate SBOMs. In this study, we demonstrate that this can be effectively bypassed by directly utilizing the lock files generated by strong PMs.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

In essence, project files and lock files serve distinct roles in dependency management. Project files serve as declarative manifests, listing only direct dependencies and their acceptable version ranges while omitting transitive dependencies. Lock files, on the other hand, are automatically generated and offer a snapshot of the dependency tree at the time of installing all dependencies, detailing all the dependencies (including transitive and indirect ones) along with the exact version used. In other words, the project file specifies how all dependencies can be resolved to build the software (e.g., version <span id="S2.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">x</span> with <span id="S2.SS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">1.2.1 \< x \< 1.5.3</span>), whereas the lock file says which specific version has been used to build a given software. This distinction is pivotal for generating accurate SBOMs, as lock files ensure precise tracking of dependencies and their versions.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.3. </span>SBOM-based Vulnerability Scanners

<div id="S2.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Typically, an SBOM-based vulnerability scanner accepts a standard SBOM report as input. Then it queries the vulnerability databases to generate a corresponding vulnerability report. For each identified vulnerability, the report provides details from sources such as the Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) database or GitHub Security Advisories (GHSA), along with specifics on how the vulnerability was matched (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2024</a>). In practice, SBOM-based scanners are integrated directly into the Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery/Deployment (CI/CD) pipeline (<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref">Shahin et al., 2017</a>). They act as automated quality gates within platforms such as GitHub Actions (<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref">GitHub, 2025a</a>) or Jenkins (<a href="#bib.bib45" class="ltx_ref">Project, 2025d</a>), scanning container images and dependencies on every code commit (<a href="#bib.bib42" class="ltx_ref">GitLab Docs, 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref">Garg, 2025</a>). This approach provides developers with immediate feedback, allowing them to fix vulnerabilities before deployment and enforcing security policies by automatically failing builds that contain high-severity issues. In this study, we select the popular scanners <span id="S2.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span> (<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">Anchore, 2025</a>) and <span id="S2.SS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> (<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Security, 2023</a>) for our evaluation.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

The Vulnerability Exploitability eXchange (VEX) (<a href="#bib.bib47" class="ltx_ref">National Telecommunications and Information Administration (NTIA), 2021</a>) has been proposed as a companion of SBOM that aims to contextualize scanner findings. A VEX statement can, for example, declare a vulnerability as <span id="S2.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">not_affected</span> if the vulnerable code is unreachable. However, VEX is merely a communication format rather than an analysis engine. It does not verify the vulnerability report, but instead, transfers the significant burden from the software consumer to the producer responsible for generating the VEX advisory. Thus, the underlying challenge of numerous false positives remains unsolved.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.4. </span>Research Landscape and Positioning

<div id="S2.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

As summarized in Table <a href="#S0.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 1 ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, recent studies evaluate SBOM generators and vulnerability scanners. We categorize them into three primary dimensions to highlight our novel contributions.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Inconsistencies in SBOM Generation:</span> Several studies highlight the inconsistent or incomplete SBOM outputs across SBOM tools (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Rabbi et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Benedetti et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cofano et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">Balliu et al., 2023</a>), Most of these evaluations rely on project manifest files (e.g., <span id="S2.SS4.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span>), which do not deterministically encode transitive dependencies. In contrast, we use lock files as the evaluation baseline and show that this choice substantially improves reproducibility and consistency.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.SS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Limitations of Current Vulnerability Scanners:</span> Existing studies (<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Benedetti et al., 2024</a>) leverage SBOM-based scanners to assess software security risks, but their conclusions are drawn without analyzing actual vulnerability reachability. However, current scanners operate purely at a coarse-grained, package level via version matching, which can inevitably overapproximate and inflate false alarms. Our results quantify this issue with a 92.0% false positive rate in our case study. This limitation also appears in binary-focused tooling such as <span id="S2.SS4.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">CVE-bin-tool</span> (<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref">Corporation, 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref">Reinhold et al., 2023</a>), where vulnerability granularity remains coarse.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S2.SS4.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Vulnerability Reachability Analysis:</span> To alleviate alert fatigue, efforts like CovSBOM (<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al., 2024</a>) integrated code coverage into <span id="S2.SS4.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Java</span> SBOMs. Inspired from this, our work provides the first multi-language empirical vulnerability check. We validate that function-call analysis prunes a considerable portion of false alarms. We also identify a key practical bottleneck: mainstream vulnerability sources (e.g., NVD, GHSA) often lack structured symbol-level metadata needed for end-to-end automation.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3. </span>Motivation

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

This section outlines the motivation for this research. Existing literature, with findings detailed in Table <a href="#S0.T1" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 1 ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, reveals that current tools often fail to generate accurate and consistent SBOMs. Therefore, this research aims to fix this deficiency by proposing reliable approaches for SBOM generation. As suggested by the NTIA, SBOMs are crucial for enhancing SSC security and can facilitate various downstream tasks. One prominent example is the detection of vulnerabilities resulting from third-party library reuse, with several studies proposing the use of SBOMs for this purpose (<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref">Anchore, 2025</a>; <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Benedetti et al., 2024</a>). Consequently, the inability of current tools to produce reliable SBOMs directly undermines the effectiveness of these critical applications, rendering downstream analyses unreliable.

</div>

<div id="S3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Specifically, <a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al. (2024)</a> systematically evaluated SBOM tools and concluded that current tools often fail to generate consistent and accurate SBOMs. Based on the findings of (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>), we conducted an in-depth analysis of two tools, <span id="S3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> (<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">Anchore, 2023</a>) and <span id="S3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> (<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Security, 2023</a>). We discovered that inconsistencies in generated SBOMs frequently stem from the reliance on project files. Certain transitive dependencies and overly broad version constraints specifications like <span id="S3.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">package \<= version</span> commonly used in Python, for instance, may not be accurately recognized, leading to inaccuracies in the resulting SBOMs. Building on our analysis of those tools, we observe that lock files, particularly those generated by strong PMs based on project files, faithfully record the exact versions of all installed dependencies, both direct and indirect. This level of complete and precise information is exactly what is required to generate accurate SBOMs, offering a reliable foundation compared to relying only on project files. Therefore, our work proposes to use these strong PMs in conjunction with their generated lockfiles to produce accurate and consistent SBOMs, thereby supporting the reliability of downstream tasks.

</div>

<div id="S3.p3" class="ltx_para">

However, as we developed this reliable method, a more fundamental and unsettling question emerged. The community has operated under the implicit assumption that an accurate SBOM is the principal missing piece for effective vulnerability management. We are motivated to challenge this assumption. We hypothesized that even with a perfect, ground-truth SBOM, the coarse, package-level analysis performed by downstream scanners might still produce unreliable results. This hypothesis formed the second, more profound motivation for our research: to empirically test the validity of the entire end-to-end, SBOM-based vulnerability scanning pipeline, an area largely taken for granted by practitioners.

</div>

<div id="S3.p4" class="ltx_para">

Therefore, the motivation for this paper is twofold. First, we aim to provide a definitive, practical solution to the known SBOM accuracy problem by prioritizing a lock-file-based approach. Second, we leverage these newly accurate SBOMs to conduct the first large-scale empirical reality check on the end-to-end vulnerability reporting pipeline. This two-stage investigation allows us not only to fix a known flaw in SBOM generation but also to expose a more critical limitation in their downstream application, thereby providing crucial insights for both researchers and practitioners.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4. </span>Methodology

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this section, we outline the methodology employed to evaluate SBOM generators and vulnerability scanners. We introduce the selected SBOM generators, PMs, and vulnerability scanners, describe the process of benchmark data collection, and define the performance metrics utilized in the evaluation. This detailed setup provides a framework for evaluating the proposed methods and the performance of SBOM tools and vulnerability scanners.

</div>

<figure id="S4.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2511.20313v2/methodology.png" id="S4.F1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:381/136;" width="381" height="136" alt="A workflow diagram of the SBOM verification pipeline, from repository collection and lock-file processing to SBOM generation, comparison with ground truth, and metric evaluation." />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 1. </span>An overview of SBOM verification methodology.<span id="acmlabel1" class="ltx_acm_description ltx_centering ltx_nodisplay">A workflow diagram of the SBOM verification pipeline, from repository collection and lock-file processing to SBOM generation, comparison with ground truth, and metric evaluation.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<figure id="S4.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2511.20313v2/VulnerableGraph.png" id="S4.F2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" style="aspect-ratio:386/146;" width="386" height="146" alt="A workflow diagram of the vulnerability verification pipeline, showing SBOM-based reporting, manual or automated reachability checking, and filtering of false positive alerts." />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2. </span>An overview of vulnerability verification methodology.<span id="acmlabel2" class="ltx_acm_description ltx_centering ltx_nodisplay">A workflow diagram of the vulnerability verification pipeline, showing SBOM-based reporting, manual or automated reachability checking, and filtering of false positive alerts.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1. </span>Overview

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Fig. <a href="#acmlabel1" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 1 ‣ 4. Methodology ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> and Fig. <a href="#acmlabel2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ 4. Methodology ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> illustrate the workflow of this study. We begin by collecting popular code repositories from GitHub and extracting their project files and lock files. For repositories that lack lock files, we use strong PMs corresponding to each programming language to generate a lock file from each project file. These newly generated lock files, along with the existing ones, serve as input for generating SBOM reports. We then compare the generated SBOMs against the ground truth data, which is extracted by parsing the lock files, to calculate Jaccard similarity and accuracy. For vulnerability detection, we first generate vulnerability reports from SBOMs generated by lock files for each repository. We then manually verify the existence of each vulnerability. Finally, we conduct a detailed analysis of the results to provide the insights discussed in Section <a href="#S6" class="ltx_ref" title="6. Discussion ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2. </span>SBOM Generators and PMs

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this work, we evaluate two popular SBOM generators: <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> (<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref">Security, 2023</a>) and <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> (<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref">Anchore, 2023</a>). <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> are open-source tools with extensive community support. Both tools support the standard CycloneDX format and support a wide range of programming languages and PM files. We select both tools to replicate consistently the method used in (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>), and to the best of our knowledge, those two are among the most widely used SBOM generators (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref">Benedetti et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Rabbi et al., 2024</a>). However, recent studies highlight that the two tools demonstrate distinct approaches for dependency management and for the resolution of transitive dependencies. Consequently, their outputs can vary significantly (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2024</a>). In this study, we employ these two generators to demonstrate that, with the appropriate input, both <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> are capable of generating identical, accurate, and complete SBOMs.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

For commonly used programming languages, we list their respective common PMs as illustrated in Table <a href="#S1.T2" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 2 ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>. The <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Lock File</span> column specifies the names of the generated lock files, while the <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Dry-Run Support</span> column indicates whether the lock file can be generated without having to install all dependencies locally, thereby conserving computational resources. The <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">SBOM</span> column denotes whether the lock file is supported by selected SBOM generators. For instance, in Rust, which employs a strong PM, the project configuration file is <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo.toml</span>, and the corresponding lock file is <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo.lock</span>, which is recognized by SBOM tools. Using the same <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo.lock</span>, hence the software environment can be reliably replicated across different machines. We focus on strong PMs that generate lock files that are compatible with SBOM tools. Accordingly, we select <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Poetry</span> for Python, <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo</span> for Rust, <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Bundler</span> for Ruby, and <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Composer</span> for PHP as the targets of our evaluation. It is worth mentioning that, although both <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.11" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.12" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> claim to support <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.13" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">package-lock.json</span>, in our experiment, they produce empty SBOMs for this file. Therefore, we consider <span id="S4.SS2.p2.1.14" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">package-lock.json</span> as unsupported by these tools <span id="footnote3" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>3</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>3</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">3</span> We conducted our experiment using <span id="footnote3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> version 0.66.0 and <span id="footnote3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> version: 1.33.0. This might not be true for future versions.</span></span></span>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

As discussed in Section <a href="#S6" class="ltx_ref" title="6. Discussion ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>, despite <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> being able to identify the same dependencies, their outputs may differ slightly due to different naming conventions. In our experimental setup, we removed platform-specific information from <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Ruby</span> dependencies to align the version conventions and scopes of both <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3. </span>Vulnerability Scan and Verification

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span> is developed by the same organization as the SBOM generator <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and is a widely-used vulnerability scanning tool that has been benchmarked in numerous studies (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Rabbi et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cofano et al., 2024</a>). <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> is a similarly popular tool capable of detecting vulnerabilities from SBOMs, and it is frequently compared with <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span> in related research (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref">Rabbi et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref">Cofano et al., 2024</a>).

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

As shown in Fig. <a href="#acmlabel2" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 2 ‣ 4. Methodology ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>, we first generate SBOMs in <span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">CycloneDX</span> format from the source code repositories using the aforementioned tools. We then use these SBOMs to generate corresponding vulnerability reports. To validate the reported vulnerabilities, we adopt the following procedure. If a vulnerability advisory explicitly identifies vulnerable functions, we first search the codebase for their occurrences and then leverage the default language server protocol (LSP) linters to analyze the corresponding function references. If no specific vulnerable functions are mentioned, we manually inspect the source code to determine whether the vulnerability is present.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

Given the time-consuming nature of this manual verification due to the poor vulnerability advisory metadata (more on this in Section <a href="#S6.SS4" class="ltx_ref" title="6.4. The Bottleneck of Unstructured Vulnerability Metadata ‣ 6. Discussion ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.4</span></a>), we adopt a systematic procedure to select four repositories per language while maintaining statistical rigor. First, we analyze the distribution of vulnerabilities reported by the scanners (detailed in the Appendix <a href="#A2" class="ltx_ref" title="Appendix B Vulnerability Distribution ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B</span></a>). For each language, our goal is to select three repositories whose vulnerability counts match the median. If fewer than three repositories hold the exact median value, we choose those with counts closest to the median. In contrast, if more than three repositories share the median vulnerability count, we further evaluate the distribution of their dependencies. From this subset, we select three repositories corresponding to the 25%, 50%, and 75% percentiles of dependency counts. Finally, to ensure broader coverage and mitigate selection bias, we randomly select an additional repository per language. This process results in four selected repositories per language, yielding a total of 16 repositories for manual inspection

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.4. </span>Data Collection

<div id="S4.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Since the authors of (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>) do not provide the list of repositories used in their study, we follow their methodology by sourcing GitHub repositories from the corresponding <span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">awesome-LANGUAGE</span> lists. These lists feature curated and popular GitHub repositories for specific programming languages. For each language, we select the <span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">awesome-LANGUAGE</span> list with the highest number of stars, as this typically indicates the list’s popularity and relevance.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Repositories within the same programming language may employ different PMs. To ensure the relevance of our dataset, we retain only those repositories that contain the aforementioned PM project files in the root directory. Although some repositories might place these files in subdirectories, GitHub enforces a limit of 5,000 API requests per user per hour for browsing files within folders. To fit within this quota, we restrict our parsing to the root directory exclusively. For repositories with multiple branches, we clone the default branch. If the repository owner specified no default branch, we prioritize the <span id="S4.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">main</span> branch, followed by the <span id="S4.SS4.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">master</span> branch. Repositories containing neither of the branches mentioned above are excluded from our dataset. For vulnerability verification, due to the lack of automated tools for large-scale validation, we randomly selected a code repository for each programming language. For each selected repository, we analyzed the two vulnerability reports derived from the SBOMs generated by <span id="S4.SS4.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span> and <span id="S4.SS4.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span>, respectively.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.5. </span>SBOM Ground Truth

<div id="S4.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Strong PMs record all installed dependencies, including transitive dependencies, within a lock file during the installation process. This lock file precisely captures the exact set of dependencies required to build the software from a clean environment at a given point in time, thereby ensuring that identical software environments can be replicated. Since SBOMs are intended to document all direct and transitive dependencies necessary for software construction, lock files provide a reliable representation of this information. Therefore, in our experiments, we use the lock files generated by strong PMs as the ground truth. For each project file, we extract the dependencies from the corresponding lock file as a set $G$ of <span id="S4.SS5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">(package, version)</span> pairs to serve as the basis for comparison.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS6" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.6. </span>Comparison Metrics

<div id="S4.SS6.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.6.1. </span>Jaccard Similarity

<div id="S4.SS6.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

For each repository, <span id="S4.SS6.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S4.SS6.SSS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> generate one SBOM each, listing the identified required software components. These SBOMs are processed to extract sets of <span id="S4.SS6.SSS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">(package, version)</span> pairs, representing the dependencies identified by each tool. To evaluate the similarity between the two sets generated from two different SBOM generators, respectively, denoted as $A$ and $B$, we employ the Jaccard similarity $J{(A,B)}$, shown in Eq. <a href="#S4.E1" class="ltx_ref" title="In 4.6.1. Jaccard Similarity ‣ 4.6. Comparison Metrics ‣ 4. Methodology ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, to quantify the overlap between the sets relative to their union.

|                                                                  |     |                                                    |     |
|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|----------------------------------------------------|-----|
| <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_left">(1)</span> |     | $${J{(A,B)}} = \frac{|{A \cap B}|}{|{A \cup B}|}$$ |     |

A higher Jaccard similarity score indicates a greater overlap between the two sets, suggesting that both tools exhibit similar behavior when processing the same input. Conversely, a lower score reflects greater divergence, highlighting differences in how the two tools parse and generate SBOM files. Ideally, if the SBOM tools adhere to the same standards for parsing and generating SBOM files from lock files, the Jaccard similarity score would be exactly equal to 1.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS6.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.6.2. </span>Accuracy Score

<div id="S4.SS6.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

For each repository, the generated SBOMs contain the software requirements. We compare the generated requirements with the ground truth, and then we calculate the accuracy of an SBOM generator. As mentioned above, we use $A$ to denote the software dependencies extracted from an SBOM and $G$ to denote the dependencies extracted from the ground truth. The accuracy is calculated as follows:

|                                                                  |     |                                           |     |
|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-------------------------------------------|-----|
| <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_left">(2)</span> |     | $${S{(A,G)}} = \frac{|{A \cap G}|}{|G|}$$ |     |

A higher accuracy score indicates that the SBOM generator has successfully captured a greater proportion of the actual software dependencies, reflecting better alignment with the ground truth. The accuracy score allows us to quantitatively evaluate the performance of SBOM generators and their ability to produce reliable SBOMs.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS6.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">4.6.3. </span>False Positive Rate

<div id="S4.SS6.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

For each vulnerability report, which contains a list of potential vulnerabilities, we manually inspect the source code repository and verify their existence. We calculate the false positive rate for each vulnerability report to measure the trustworthiness of the vulnerability scanner. We use $V_{r}$ to denote the total number of vulnerabilities reported, and $V_{c}$ to denote the number of vulnerabilities that are included in $V_{r}$ and confirmed in the source code. The false positive rate is calculated as follows:

|                                                                  |     |                                                      |     |
|------------------------------------------------------------------|-----|------------------------------------------------------|-----|
| <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_left">(3)</span> |     | $${F{(V_{r},V_{c})}} = \frac{V_{r} - V_{c}}{V_{r}}$$ |     |

A lower false positive rate indicates that the vulnerability scanner is more precise in correctly identifying genuine security vulnerabilities, while minimizing the number of incorrectly flagged issues. In addition, a low false positive rate tool produces more actionable reports and reduces the manual effort required by developers to verify and triage alerts. Ideally, if vulnerability reports identify all vulnerabilities correctly, the false positive rate would be 0.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5. </span>Evaluation

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this section, we describe our dataset and evaluate the performance of SBOM tools with lock files as input. We aim to answer the following evaluation questions (EQs):

</div>

<div id="S5.p2" class="ltx_para">

1.  <span id="S5.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">(1)</span></span>
    <div id="S5.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

    <span id="S5.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ 1:</span> Can we generate a lock file for the projects containing only a project file?

    </div>
2.  <span id="S5.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">(2)</span></span>
    <div id="S5.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

    <span id="S5.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ 2:</span> Starting from the same lock file, will different SBOM tools generate <span id="S5.I1.i2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">consistent</span> results from lock files?

    </div>
3.  <span id="S5.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">(3)</span></span>
    <div id="S5.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

    <span id="S5.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ 3:</span> Can SBOM tools generate <span id="S5.I1.i3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">accurate</span> results from lock files?

    </div>
4.  <span id="S5.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">(4)</span></span>
    <div id="S5.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

    <span id="S5.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ 4:</span> Starting from accurate SBOMs, can vulnerability scanners generate <span id="S5.I1.i4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">accurate</span> vulnerability reports?

    </div>

</div>

<div id="S5.p3" class="ltx_para">

To answer <span id="S5.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ1</span>, we first evaluate the feasibility of generating lock files for repositories that rely solely on project files. Using the resulting set of lock files from both pre-existing and newly generated, we address <span id="S5.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ2</span> by assessing the consistency between <span id="S5.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> via Jaccard similarity scores. For <span id="S5.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ3</span>, we establish the ground truth by directly parsing the lock files and measuring the accuracy of the generated SBOMs against this baseline. Finally, regarding <span id="S5.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ4</span>, we feed these verified, high-fidelity SBOMs into vulnerability scanners and manually verify the reported vulnerabilities against the source code to determine the false positive rate.

</div>

<div id="S5.p4" class="ltx_para">

It is worth noting that both CycloneDX and SPDX are supported as output formats by <span id="S5.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span>, and they produce identical results, modulo their syntax. Therefore, the results presented in this section are equally true when using either one of those standards as input for the evaluation. Our experiments demonstrate that, even with project files only, generating lock files from project files allows SBOM tools to achieve highly accurate results. These findings highlight that utilizing lock files as input is the best practice for SBOM tools. To further illustrate this, we conduct a case study as a concrete example (Section <a href="#S5.SS6" class="ltx_ref" title="5.6. Case Study: Transition from Weak to Strong PM ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.6</span></a>) and discuss related threats to validity in Section <a href="#S6.SS1" class="ltx_ref" title="6.1. Threats to Validity ‣ 6. Discussion ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.1</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.1. </span>Dataset Description

</figure>

<div id="S5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our dataset comprises 2,414 repositories spanning four different PMs and their associated programming languages. Table <a href="#S5.T3" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 3 ‣ 5.1. Dataset Description ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> describes the statistics about our dataset. <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Project File Only</span> indicates that there is only the project file found in the root folder of that repository, whereas <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Both</span> indicates that both the project file and the corresponding lock file are available. It is worth noting that <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Poetry</span> is an unofficial PM, and Python supports several other popular PMs, including <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Pip</span> and <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Setuptools</span> (both weak PMs) and <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Pipenv</span> (strong PM). Hence, the number of Python repositories using <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Poetry</span> is relatively lower compared to other languages. In contrast, Rust uses <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo</span>, its official and sole PM, leading to the largest number of repositories depending on it. Finally, the relatively lower popularity of Ruby and PHP compared to other languages contributes to their smaller repository counts.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.2. </span>Feasibility of Lock File Generation

<div id="S5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Since strong PMs provide native support for lock file generation, we evaluate whether lock files can be reconstructed for repositories that only contain project files but lack existing lock files. For this purpose, we develop automated scripts that invoke the corresponding PMs to generate lock files directly from project files.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

As shown in Table <a href="#S5.T4" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 4 ‣ 5.2. Feasibility of Lock File Generation ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>, lock file generation is highly successful for <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Rust</span>, where over 96% of repositories can be resolved automatically. Manual inspection of the failed cases indicates that most failures stem from legacy dependencies that are no longer resolvable under the current package registry state (e.g., removed versions or changed package name). Similarly, due to outdated versions of <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">PHP</span> and <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Ruby</span> being installed in our experimental environment, many software projects specify newer version constraints for <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">PHP</span> and <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Ruby</span>, which our automated scripts could not accommodate smartly, resulting in errors. However, these issues can be resolved by manually installing the required updated versions and toolchains.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

In contrast, Python presents a unique challenge, with a success rate of 19.1%. This lower automated conversion rate highlights the fragmentation of the Python build ecosystem rather than an inability to generate lock files. Although <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pyproject.toml</span> is the standard configuration (<a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref">Cannon and Smith, 2016</a>), it supports diverse build backends (e.g., <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">setuptools</span>, <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">flit</span>) that are not universally compatible with a single strong PM like <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Poetry</span>. In our experiment, we strictly employed <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Poetry</span> to maintain consistency. As a consequence, our automated methodology compared to other languages results in a higher failure rate. For practitioners, these cases can be resolved by selecting backend-compatible tools (e.g., <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">uv</span> or <span id="S5.SS2.p3.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pip-tools</span>), as we discuss in Section <a href="#S6" class="ltx_ref" title="6. Discussion ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>.

</div>

<figure id="S5.T4" class="ltx_table">
<table id="S5.T4.2" class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_align_middle">
<tbody>
<tr id="S5.T4.2.1" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="S5.T4.2.1.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_tt"><span id="S5.T4.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Language</span></td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.1.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S5.T4.2.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Successful</span></td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.1.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S5.T4.2.1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Failed</span></td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.1.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S5.T4.2.1.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Success Rate</span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="S5.T4.2.2" class="even ltx_tr">
<td id="S5.T4.2.2.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_t">Python</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.2.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t">42</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.2.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t">178</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.2.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_t">19.1%</td>
</tr>
<tr id="S5.T4.2.3" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="S5.T4.2.3.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left">Rust</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.3.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">532</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.3.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">20</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.3.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">96.4%</td>
</tr>
<tr id="S5.T4.2.4" class="even ltx_tr">
<td id="S5.T4.2.4.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left">Ruby</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.4.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">456</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.4.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">142</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.4.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center">76.3%</td>
</tr>
<tr id="S5.T4.2.5" class="odd ltx_tr">
<td id="S5.T4.2.5.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_bb">PHP</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.5.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb">165</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.5.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb">138</td>
<td id="S5.T4.2.5.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb">54.5%</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 4. </span>Lock file generation across different programming languages.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

It is worth noting that for repositories with pre-existing lock files, we also verified the capability to regenerate them from project files to ensure dependency freshness. We apply the same automation pipeline to these repositories. The total number of usable lock files per language is reported in Table <a href="#S5.T5" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 5 ‣ 5.4. Accuracy against Ground Truth ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>. Using the resulting lock files, we generate SBOMs with <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.SS2.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> for downstream analysis.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzUuU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxNTQuMjUiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCA0NzcuMzggMTU0LjI1IiB3aWR0aD0iNDc3LjM4Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDE1NC4yNSkgbWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgMCkiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDAgOS40MSBMIDAgMTQ0Ljg0IEMgMCAxNTAuMDQgNC4yMSAxNTQuMjUgOS40MSAxNTQuMjUgTCA0NjcuOTcgMTU0LjI1IEMgNDczLjE2IDE1NC4yNSA0NzcuMzggMTUwLjA0IDQ3Ny4zOCAxNDQuODQgTCA0NzcuMzggOS40MSBDIDQ3Ny4zOCA0LjIxIDQ3My4xNiAwIDQ2Ny45NyAwIEwgOS40MSAwIEMgNC4yMSAwIDAgNC4yMSAwIDkuNDEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6I0YyRjJGMjsiIGZpbGw9IiNGMkYyRjIiIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIj48cGF0aCBzdHlsZT0ic3Ryb2tlOm5vbmUiIGQ9Ik0gMS4xMSA5LjQxIEwgMS4xMSAxNDQuODQgQyAxLjExIDE0OS40MyA0LjgyIDE1My4xNCA5LjQxIDE1My4xNCBMIDQ2Ny45NyAxNTMuMTQgQyA0NzIuNTUgMTUzLjE0IDQ3Ni4yNyAxNDkuNDMgNDc2LjI3IDE0NC44NCBMIDQ3Ni4yNyA5LjQxIEMgNDc2LjI3IDQuODIgNDcyLjU1IDEuMTEgNDY3Ljk3IDEuMTEgTCA5LjQxIDEuMTEgQyA0LjgyIDEuMTEgMS4xMSA0LjgyIDEuMTEgOS40MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIDEyLjkyIDE1LjYxKSI+PGZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Qgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZvLXdpZHRoOjMyLjYzZW07LS1sdHgtZm8taGVpZ2h0OjkuMDllbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowLjE5ZW07Zm9udC1zaXplOjEwcHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEyOC40MiIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgMTI1LjczKSIgd2lkdGg9IjQ1MS41MSI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iUzUuU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9pbmxpbmUtYmxvY2sgbHR4X21pbmlwYWdlIGx0eF9hbGlnbl9ib3R0b20iIHN0eWxlPSJ3aWR0aDozMi42M2VtOyI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNS5TUzIucDUucGljMS4xLjEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcCI+PHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM1LlNTMi5wNS5waWMxLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ib </div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.3. </span>Consistency Across SBOM Tools

<div id="S5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate the consistency of SBOM generation, we use the existing lock files harvested from our dataset as the primary input for both <span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span>. We then compute the Jaccard similarity coefficient to quantify the agreement between the dependency sets identified by each tool.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

The results are definitive: we observed a <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">perfect Jaccard similarity score of 1.0</span> with <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">zero variance</span> across all tested repositories in <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Python</span>, <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Rust</span>, <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Ruby</span>, and <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">PHP</span>. This stands in clear contrast to prior studies (<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref">Yu et al., 2024</a>; <a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref">O’Donoghue et al., 2023</a>) that reported significant discrepancies when relying on project files. Our findings demonstrate, thanks to a large scale evaluation, that lock files effectively eliminate the ambiguity associated with dependency resolution. Consequently, when provided with this deterministic input, different SBOM generators are capable of producing identical, high-fidelity outputs. Based on this evidence, we answer <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ2</span> as follows:

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzUuU1MzLnAzLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDEuNzUiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCA0NzcuMzggMTAxLjc1IiB3aWR0aD0iNDc3LjM4Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEwMS43NSkgbWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgMCkiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDAgOS40MSBMIDAgOTIuMzQgQyAwIDk3LjUzIDQuMjEgMTAxLjc1IDkuNDEgMTAxLjc1IEwgNDY3Ljk3IDEwMS43NSBDIDQ3My4xNiAxMDEuNzUgNDc3LjM4IDk3LjUzIDQ3Ny4zOCA5Mi4zNCBMIDQ3Ny4zOCA5LjQxIEMgNDc3LjM4IDQuMjEgNDczLjE2IDAgNDY3Ljk3IDAgTCA5LjQxIDAgQyA0LjIxIDAgMCA0LjIxIDAgOS40MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojRjJGMkYyOyIgZmlsbD0iI0YyRjJGMiIgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiPjxwYXRoIHN0eWxlPSJzdHJva2U6bm9uZSIgZD0iTSAxLjExIDkuNDEgTCAxLjExIDkyLjM0IEMgMS4xMSA5Ni45MiA0LjgyIDEwMC42NCA5LjQxIDEwMC42NCBMIDQ2Ny45NyAxMDAuNjQgQyA0NzIuNTUgMTAwLjY0IDQ3Ni4yNyA5Ni45MiA0NzYuMjcgOTIuMzQgTCA0NzYuMjcgOS40MSBDIDQ3Ni4yNyA0LjgyIDQ3Mi41NSAxLjExIDQ2Ny45NyAxLjExIEwgOS40MSAxLjExIEMgNC44MiAxLjExIDEuMTEgNC44MiAxLjExIDkuNDEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCAxMi45MiAxMi45MikiPjxmb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0IHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1mby13aWR0aDozMi42M2VtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWhlaWdodDo1LjQ5ZW07LS1sdHgtZm8tZGVwdGg6MGVtO2ZvbnQtc2l6ZToxMHB0OyIgaGVpZ2h0PSI3NS45MSIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgNzUuOTEpIiB3aWR0aD0iNDUxLjUxIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNS5TUzMucDMucGljMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBsdHhfbWluaXBhZ2UgbHR4X2FsaWduX2JvdHRvbSIgc3R5bGU9IndpZHRoOjMyLjYzZW07Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM1LlNTMy5wMy5waWMxLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9wIj48c3BhbiBpZD0iUzUuU1MzLnAzLnBpYzEuMS4xLjEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfdGV4dCBsdHhfZ id="S5.SS3.p3.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.4. </span>Accuracy against Ground Truth

<div id="S5.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

The consistency between <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> alone does not confirm the reliability of the SBOMs they generate, as it fails to indicate whether both tools produce accurate or inaccurate results. Consequently, we validate the generated SBOMs against a ground truth, which is obtained from the lock files, to determine their correctness. Since <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> produce the same result when using each file, their results are grouped together in a single column for comparison. Table <a href="#S5.T5" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 5 ‣ 5.4. Accuracy against Ground Truth ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> presents the experimental results. The <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Trivy & Syft</span> column lists dependencies uniquely identified by these tools, while the <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Ground Truth</span> column shows dependencies present in the ground truth but missing from the SBOMs. The <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Overlap</span> column indicates dependencies found in both the SBOMs and the ground truth, and the <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Accuracy</span> column is derived from these values. From the results, we conclude our answer to <span id="S5.SS4.p1.1.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">EQ3</span>.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<div id="S5.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

It is important to note that this study focuses exclusively on the correctness of the <span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">name</span> and <span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">version</span> of each component. As discussed in Section <a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref" title="2. Background ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>, SBOMs also encompass additional details such as <span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">license</span>, <span id="S5.SS4.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">manufacturer</span>, and other metadata. Since lock files do not include this information, generating a complete and accurate SBOM requires more than simply referencing the lock file.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.5. </span>Vulnerability Report Evaluation

<div id="S5.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Using the SBOMs from the previous experiment, we evaluate the performance of two popular SBOM-based vulnerability scanners: <span id="S5.SS5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="S5.SS5.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span>. To adhere to the recommended workflow for each tool, we generated two distinct SBOMs from the lock file of each code repository. The first SBOM was generated with <span id="S5.SS5.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and subsequently scanned by its companion tool, <span id="S5.SS5.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span>. The second SBOM was generated and scanned using <span id="S5.SS5.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span>. This paired approach ensures that each scanner operates on an SBOM created by its corresponding generation tool, aligning with the developer’s recommendations.

</div>

<td id="S5.T6.2.1.7.1" class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="S5.T6.2.1.7.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Total</span></td>
<td id="S5.T6.2.1.7.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="S5.T6.2.1.7.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">496</span></td>
<td id="S5.T6.2.1.7.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="S5.T6.2.1.7.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">5615</span></td>
<td id="S5.T6.2.1.7.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="S5.T6.2.1.7.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">11.32</span></td>
<td id="S5.T6.2.1.7.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="S5.T6.2.1.7.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">486</span></td>
<td id="S5.T6.2.1.7.6" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="S5.T6.2.1.7.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">5555</span></td>
<td id="S5.T6.2.1.7.7" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="S5.T6.2.1.7.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">11.43</span></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 6. </span>Comparison of vulnerabilities detected by Grype and Trivy.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS5.p2" class="ltx_para">

Initially, we scanned all our code repositories for vulnerabilities using both SBOM-based scanners, and the results are summarized in Table <a href="#S5.T6" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 6 ‣ 5.5. Vulnerability Report Evaluation ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>. Some repositories do not rely on any third-party libraries and therefore cannot yield vulnerability findings. Consequently, the <span id="S5.SS5.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">No. Repos</span> column reports only the number of repositories in which at least one vulnerability was identified, while the <span id="S5.SS5.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">No. Vulns</span> column gives the total count of vulnerabilities detected across them. On average, vulnerable repositories contain more than three vulnerabilities each, with <span id="S5.SS5.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Ruby</span> projects showing the highest density at over 20 per repository.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS5.p3" class="ltx_para">

Although the large number of vulnerabilities per vulnerable repository might raise a significant and widespread security concern, they do not distinguish between true and false positives and, therefore, cannot represent the practical trustworthiness of the scanners. To further investigate this, we conduct a follow-up validation experiment. However, there is an absence of automated source code analysis tools for our target languages, unlike specialized tools such as <a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref">Zhao et al. (2024)</a> for <span id="S5.SS5.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Java</span>. Hence, we transition from our previous large-scale analysis to a deep-dive manual validation, which is inherently labor-intensive. Therefore, we prioritize a deep-dive analysis on a small and representative set of repositories over a broader but superficial statistical count. Accordingly, we randomly select one repository that contains fewer than 20 vulnerabilities reported for each language, scan it with both scanners, <span id="S5.SS5.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span> and <span id="S5.SS5.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span>, and then manually inspect the source code to validate each reported vulnerability.

</div>

<td id="S5.T7.4.1.19.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb"><span id="S5.T7.4.1.19.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">42</span></td>
<td id="S5.T7.4.1.19.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb"><span id="S5.T7.4.1.19.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">9</span></td>
<td id="S5.T7.4.1.19.5" class="ltx_td ltx_align_right ltx_border_bb"><span id="S5.T7.4.1.19.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">78.6%</span></td>
