<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 7. </span>Reported vs. confirmed vulnerabilities. <span class="math inline"><em>V</em><sub><em>c</em></sub></span> is confirmed if the code <span id="S5.T7.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">exists</span> in the repository. <span class="math inline"><em>V</em><sub><em>r</em></sub></span>: reported, <span class="math inline"><em>V</em><sub><em>c</em></sub></span>: confirmed, FPR: false positive rate.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS5.p4" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S5.T7" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 7 ‣ 5.5. Vulnerability Report Evaluation ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> presents the results of our manual validation, which revealed an exceptionally high false positive rate. The $V_{r}$ in Table <a href="#S5.T7" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 7 ‣ 5.5. Vulnerability Report Evaluation ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a> represents the union of findings from both <span id="S5.SS5.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span> and <span id="S5.SS5.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span>. It is important to note that, starting from the same accurate SBOM, we observed a near-perfect agreement between the two scanners. Despite the fact that these tools utilize distinct vulnerability databases. From the 113 potential vulnerabilities reported across four repositories, our in-depth manual inspection confirmed only 9 true positives<span id="footnote4" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>4</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>4</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">4</span> Following ethical security research practices, we have responsibly disclosed these findings to the respective maintainers. Several authors have already acknowledged and addressed these issues.</span></span></span>. This corresponds to a staggering overall false positive rate of 92.0%. This result demonstrates a pronounced discrepancy between automated reports and actual security risks. Unfortunately, facing such a high false positive rate, we cannot help questioning the usability and trustworthiness of their methods.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS5.p5" class="ltx_para">

We notice that a substantial portion of the vulnerabilities detected are not from developer-introduced third-party dependencies, but from the foundational runtime libraries of the programming languages themselves. For example, the scanners flagged ten vulnerabilities within the <span id="S5.SS5.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">activesupport@3.0.1</span> library. As a core dependency of <span id="S5.SS5.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Ruby on Rails</span>, <span id="S5.SS5.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">activesupport</span> is a comprehensive library offering a vast collection of functionalities. The vulnerability reports almost exclusively cited issues related to HTTP-based attacks. However, this repository only invoked a very small and isolated subset of <span id="S5.SS5.p5.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">activesupport</span>’s capabilities. A manual inspection confirmed that the vulnerable modules were never imported or referred, rendering all ten reported vulnerabilities untriggerable and, therefore, not applicable to the security posture of this specific application.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS5.p6" class="ltx_para">

Our evaluation strongly indicates a systemic limitation in the current generation of SBOM scanners rather than an implementation defect. The fundamental issue is that these tools primarily operate at the package level, verifying the presence of vulnerable library versions listed in dependency manifests without analyzing the actual function call. To validate this hypothesis, we conducted a further experiment by analyzing whether the specific vulnerable functions cited in the reports were ever actually used by the code. The results, presented in the $T\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}t\hspace{0pt}a\hspace{0pt}l_{c\hspace{0pt}o\hspace{0pt}v}$ row of Table <a href="#S5.T7" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 7 ‣ 5.5. Vulnerability Report Evaluation ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>, show that the coverage-based analysis successfully eliminated 61.9% reported false vulnerabilities, reducing the number of alerts requiring manual inspection from 113 to 42. It demonstrates that a significant portion of false positives can be systematically filtered out. This lack of context awareness generates a significant volume of false positives, leading to substantial alert fatigue. As developers become inundated with alerts, they must manually check each one, a process that not only misdirects engineering efforts but also fosters an environment where critical, exploitable vulnerabilities are more likely to be overlooked in tons of false alarms. As a consequence, paradoxically, this noisy security approach can ultimately harm software security. The effectiveness of our call analysis strongly suggests the value of developing coverage-based SBOM vulnerability scanners for SSC security ecosystems.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS5.p7" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iUzUuU1M1LnA3LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMzcuNjUiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCA0NzcuMzggMTM3LjY1IiB3aWR0aD0iNDc3LjM4Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEzNy42NSkgbWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgMCkiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDAgOS40MSBMIDAgMTI4LjI0IEMgMCAxMzMuNDMgNC4yMSAxMzcuNjUgOS40MSAxMzcuNjUgTCA0NjcuOTcgMTM3LjY1IEMgNDczLjE2IDEzNy42NSA0NzcuMzggMTMzLjQzIDQ3Ny4zOCAxMjguMjQgTCA0NzcuMzggOS40MSBDIDQ3Ny4zOCA0LjIxIDQ3My4xNiAwIDQ2Ny45NyAwIEwgOS40MSAwIEMgNC4yMSAwIDAgNC4yMSAwIDkuNDEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6I0YyRjJGMjsiIGZpbGw9IiNGMkYyRjIiIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIj48cGF0aCBzdHlsZT0ic3Ryb2tlOm5vbmUiIGQ9Ik0gMS4xMSA5LjQxIEwgMS4xMSAxMjguMjQgQyAxLjExIDEzMi44MiA0LjgyIDEzNi41NCA5LjQxIDEzNi41NCBMIDQ2Ny45NyAxMzYuNTQgQyA0NzIuNTUgMTM2LjU0IDQ3Ni4yNyAxMzIuODIgNDc2LjI3IDEyOC4yNCBMIDQ3Ni4yNyA5LjQxIEMgNDc2LjI3IDQuODIgNDcyLjU1IDEuMTEgNDY3Ljk3IDEuMTEgTCA5LjQxIDEuMTEgQyA0LjgyIDEuMTEgMS4xMSA0LjgyIDEuMTEgOS40MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIDEyLjkyIDE1LjYxKSI+PGZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Qgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZvLXdpZHRoOjMyLjYzZW07LS1sdHgtZm8taGVpZ2h0OjcuODllbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowLjE5ZW07Zm9udC1zaXplOjEwcHQ7IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjExMS44MSIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgMTA5LjEyKSIgd2lkdGg9IjQ1MS41MSI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iUzUuU1M1LnA3LnBpYzEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9pbmxpbmUtYmxvY2sgbHR4X21pbmlwYWdlIGx0eF9hbGlnbl9ib3R0b20iIHN0eWxlPSJ3aWR0aDozMi42M2VtOyI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJTNS5TUzUucDcucGljMS4xLjEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcCI+PHNwYW4gaWQ9IlM1LlNTNS5wNy5waWMxLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ib id="S5.SS5.p7.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS6" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.6. </span>Case Study: Transition from Weak to Strong PM

<div id="S5.SS6.p1" class="ltx_para">

We present a concrete real-world case demonstrating how to generate an accurate SBOM using a lock file from a repository managed by a weak PM. Specifically, we analyze the <span id="S5.SS6.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gym</span> repository from <span id="S5.SS6.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">OpenAI</span>’s GitHub repository. <span id="S5.SS6.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gym</span> is a widely used Python library for reinforcement learning, with over 1,315 dependent packages and 60,881 third-party projects relying on it, making it one of the most popular reinforcement learning libraries (<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref">The Farama Foundation, 2022</a>). However, prioritizing native compatibility, <span id="S5.SS6.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gym</span> employs the weak PM <span id="S5.SS6.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pip</span>, the official PM of <span id="S5.SS6.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Python</span>, and uses <span id="S5.SS6.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span> to manage its dependencies. As discussed earlier, this project file does not capture indirect dependencies, potentially leading SBOM tools to produce inaccurate results.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS6.p2" class="ltx_para">

We use the <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span> file as the input for both of the previously mentioned SBOM generators. In the <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span> file, 15 dependencies are explicitly listed. However, both <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> detect only 5 dependencies. Further investigation reveals that these detected dependencies are constrained strictly by the <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">==</span> operator, while dependencies specified with a version range (e.g., <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">\>=</span>) are not identified. Moreover, a discrepancy is observed between the two tools. In the <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span> file, the dependency <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">typing_extensions</span> is altered to <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.9" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">typing-extensions</span> by <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span>, whereas <span id="S5.SS6.p2.1.11" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> retains the original format.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS6.p3" class="ltx_para">

Following the workflow mentioned above, we parse the <span id="S5.SS6.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">require ments.txt</span> file and transfer the dependencies, along with their corresponding version constraints, into the <span id="S5.SS6.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pyproject.toml</span> file, which is the project file for the strong PM <span id="S5.SS6.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Poetry</span>. Subsequently, we generate the corresponding lock file and use both <span id="S5.SS6.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S5.SS6.p3.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> to construct an SBOM based on this lock file and compare the results. In total, 32 dependencies are now identified consistently. Based on the newly generated SBOM file, we conduct a vulnerability scan and no vulnerabilities are found in this repository.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS6.p4" class="ltx_para">

This use case shows how easily we can generate an accurate SBOM and conduct a vulnerability scan even when a certain project uses a weak PM and project file to manage its dependencies. Our experiment demonstrates that bridging it to a strong PM flow (as detailed in our migration guidelines in Section <a href="#S6.SS3" class="ltx_ref" title="6.3. Transition From Weak to Strong PM ‣ 6. Discussion ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6.3</span></a>) effectively eliminates the noise caused by incomplete dependency resolution. In this case study, SBOM tools produce identical and accurate reports that match the ground truth, and the subsequent vulnerability scan validates the security of the repository, with no vulnerabilities detected.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS7" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.7. </span>Feasibility of Automated Reachability Analysis

<div id="S5.SS7.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our manual verification in Section <a href="#S5.SS5" class="ltx_ref" title="5.5. Vulnerability Report Evaluation ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.5</span></a> exposes a critical issue that existing scanners produce excessive false positives. Scaling this verification to a larger dataset, however, is restricted by a data limitation. Most vulnerability advisories (e.g., CVE, GitHub Advisory) lack machine-readable <span id="S5.SS7.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">affected symbol</span> metadata, making manual inspection the only viable option.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS7.p2" class="ltx_para">

We use the Go ecosystem to validate the feasibility of automating this process. Unlike others, the Go vulnerability database explicitly provides structured symbol data. Leveraging this, we implement our Proof-of-Concept (PoC) automated tool<a href="#footnote2" class="ltx_ref" title="footnote 2 ‣ 3rd item ‣ 1. Introduction ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> to perform reachability analysis following the methods mentioned before and evaluate it on 12 popular Go projects. We show that even a lightweight, conservative call reachability analysis can eliminate a substantial fraction of evidently irrelevant vulnerability reports.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS7.p3" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S5.T8" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 8 ‣ 5.7. Feasibility of Automated Reachability Analysis ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> summarizes the results (We demonstrate the detailed breakdown in Appendix A). Out of 121 reported vulnerabilities, our automated analysis confirm that only 31 were present, representing a <span id="S5.SS7.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">74.4% reduction</span> in false positives. The improvement is particularly significant for complex projects. For instance, we reduce 90% reported vulnerabilities in <span id="S5.SS7.p3.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Prometheus</span>. We conclude that if upstream advisories provide granular metadata, reachability analysis can be fully automated beyond <span id="S5.SS7.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Go</span> ecosystem. Furthermore, we can significantly reduce the false positive rate and alleviate the developers’ alert fatigue.

</div>

<figure id="S5.T8" class="ltx_table">
<div id="S5.T8.2" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_center ltx_transformed_outer" style="width:345.0pt;height:24.3pt;vertical-align:-9.7pt;">
<span class="ltx_transformed_inner" style="transform:translate(-6.4pt,0.5pt) scale(0.964267678754685,0.964267678754685) ;"> </span>
<table id="S5.T8.2.1" class="ltx_tabular ltx_align_middle">
<tbody>
<tr id="S5.T8.2.1.1" class="odd ltx_tr">

<td id="S5.T8.2.1.1.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S5.T8.2.1.1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Total Reported</span></td>
<td id="S5.T8.2.1.1.3" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S5.T8.2.1.1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Confirmed Reachable</span></td>
<td id="S5.T8.2.1.1.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_tt"><span id="S5.T8.2.1.1.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Reduction Rate</span></td>
</tr>

</tr>

</div>

</figure>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6. </span>Discussion

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this section, we first discuss the threats to validity of our study. We then introduce the reasons behind the differences observed in our SBOM evaluation and how to transition from weak PMs. Subsequently, we outline best practices to enhance the security of SSC and the reliability of SBOM tools.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.1. </span>Threats to Validity

<div id="S6.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

For the SBOM experiment, we construct our ground truth based on the lock files generated by strong PMs. However, our experimental setup is limited to a fixed environment. It is possible that these PMs produce inconsistent dependencies across different environments, such as varying operating systems, CPU architectures, and other system configurations. SBOMs tailored to specific runtime environments may offer greater accuracy, which can be a direction for future exploration. Regarding vulnerability verification, our manual inspection process is subject to human error. It is possible that certain vulnerabilities were inadvertently overlooked or that some could be exploited through novel or complex attack vectors beyond the scope of our analysis. Our manual verification is limited to 4 repositories due to the labor-intensive nature of deep code auditing and function call tracing. However, the root causes of false positives proved remarkably consistent across diverse languages. Consequently, extending the manual effort would yield diminishing returns. We prioritized a granular, deep-dive analysis over a broader but superficial statistical count to ensure high-fidelity insights Our study focuses solely on static analysis under normal software development conditions. Consequently, techniques such as obfuscation, reflection, dynamic code loading, and dependency vendoring (<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref">Birsan, 2021</a>; <a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref">Miller and others, 2023</a>; <a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref">Qu et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#bib.bib49" class="ltx_ref">Cox, 2018</a>) are not addressed, which may limit the generalizability of our results.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.2. </span>Factors Contributing to SBOM Discrepancies

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.2.1. </span>Undefined Behavior

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Although SBOM standards are publicly available, the implementation of SBOM tools may vary. Differences in how those tools handle the input files can result in inconsistent outputs. For instance, when generating SBOMs from <span id="S6.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">poetry.lock</span>, we observe that if a package in the lock file has the attribute <span id="S6.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">category</span> set to <span id="S6.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">dev</span>, early versions of <span id="S6.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> (before <span id="S6.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">v0.59.1</span>) omit the package, while <span id="S6.SS2.SSS1.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> includes it in the SBOM. While the latest versions mitigate this issue, a systematic solution requires standardized behavior.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.2.2. </span>Incomplete Dependency Resolve

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

SBOM tools may fail to resolve all dependencies, leading to incomplete SBOMs. For example, when generating SBOMs from <span id="S6.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span> files, both <span id="S6.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="S6.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> only resolved direct dependencies explicitly specified in the <span id="S6.SS2.SSS2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">package==version</span>-like format, resulting in incomplete and inconsistent SBOMs. This issue can be mitigated by using lock files, which comprehensively record all dependencies, including transitive ones, and specify exact versions. This ensures that the generated SBOMs are both complete and accurate.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.2.3. </span>Inconsistent Naming Convention

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Dependency naming conventions may differ across PMs, leading to inconsistencies in the generated SBOMs. For example, when generating SBOMs from <span id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemfile.lock</span>, we find discrepancies in how <span id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> name dependencies. Ruby’s <span id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemfile.lock</span> may include dependencies with platform-specific annotations, such as <span id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">sqlite3 (2.0.2-aarch 64-linux-gnu)</span>. <span id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> outputs only the name and version, omitting the architecture, while <span id="S6.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> appends the architecture after the version as part of the full version name. These differences result in inconsistent dependency representations, which can affect subsequent analyses. Standardizing dependency naming across PMs can address this issue and ensure consistent SBOM generation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS4" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.2.4. </span>Directory Scanning Behavior

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

When provided with a directory as input, SBOM tools often scan the entire directory recursively to identify project files for analysis. However, discrepancies in scanning behavior can lead to inconsistent results. For instance, some tools include auxiliary folders such as <span id="S6.SS2.SSS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">.ci</span>, <span id="S6.SS2.SSS4.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">test</span>, or <span id="S6.SS2.SSS4.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">docker</span>, in addition to the <span id="S6.SS2.SSS4.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">GitHub Actions</span> folder, while others exclude them. These folders are not the software’s direct dependencies but are used for CI/CD pipelines or release processes. The inconsistency in whether these auxiliary folders are included during scanning contributes to differences in the generated SBOMs (<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref">Balliu et al., 2023</a>).

</div>

<div id="S6.SS2.SSS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Additionally, other factors may exacerbate inconsistencies in SBOM generation. For instance, implementation errors in the tools themselves, such as incorrect handling of file paths or misinterpretation of configuration files, can introduce inaccuracies. Similarly, issues with parsing lock files, such as failing to recognize all dependencies or incorrectly resolving transitive dependencies, can further affect the accuracy of the SBOMs. These challenges highlight the need for standardization in the way SBOM tools scan directories and interpret project metadata to ensure reliable and consistent results.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.3. </span>Transition From Weak to Strong PM

<div id="S6.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our results clearly indicate that migrating to strong PMs, that produce lock files, is imperative for accurate SBOM generation and subsequent security analysis. We propose a practical migration guideline addressing two common scenarios found in legacy codebases. For Python, the fragmented ecosystem and the prevalence of its default weak PM necessitate manual effort for developers to migrate toward strong alternatives. In contrast, ecosystems such as Rust, Ruby, and PHP benefit from official package managers that function as strong PMs by default. In these cases, the primary barrier to robust SBOM generation is user adoption rather than a lack of tooling capability.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.3.1. </span>Bridging the Gap for Weak PMs.

<div id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

For projects adopting weak PMs (e.g., Python’s <span id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pip</span> with <span id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span>), completely refactoring to a strong PM format (e.g., <span id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Poetry</span>’s <span id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pyproject.toml</span>) can be labor-intensive. To mitigate this migration cost, we recommend utilizing modern bridge tools that offer interoperability. For example, the emerging tool <span id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">uv</span> (<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref">astral-sh, 2025</a>) allows developers to treat <span id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">requirements.txt</span> as a source manifest and employs a <span id="S6.SS3.SSS1.p1.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pip-compile</span> to resolve dependencies deterministically. This process generates a lock file that captures the exact dependency tree, enabling the generation of accurate SBOMs without forcing a disruptive overhaul of the existing project structure.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS3.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.3.2. </span>Producing Lock Files in Strong PMs.

<div id="S6.SS3.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

For repositories already utilizing strong PMs (e.g., Rust’s <span id="S6.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo</span>) but failing to track lock files in version control, the solution is procedural rather than technical. Developers must integrate lock file generation into the build pipeline and commit these files as the source of truth. Modern strong PMs facilitate this efficiently; for instance, <span id="S6.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo</span> supports a <span id="S6.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">dry-run</span> mechanism that resolves the dependency graph and updates the <span id="S6.SS3.SSS2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Cargo.lock</span> file without triggering the resource-intensive download and installation of artifacts. This ensures high-fidelity SBOM generation with minimal overhead.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS3.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

Ultimately, facilitating this transition is not merely an operational convenience but a security necessity. By standardizing on lock-file-based generation through these migration paths, we establish the high-fidelity ground truth required to eliminate upstream noise, thereby significantly enhancing the reliability of the entire downstream vulnerability management lifecycle.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.4. </span>The Bottleneck of Unstructured Vulnerability Metadata

<div id="S6.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our automated analysis in the Go ecosystem shows that automated reachability verification is feasible when the vulnerability metadata is available, reducing considerable false positives. However, unlike the <span id="S6.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Go</span> ecosystem, for other languages, information about affected functions is typically buried in unstructured natural language text. Consequently, automating reachability analysis for these languages without high-fidelity, machine-readable metadata is highly impractical to execute reliably at scale, thereby restricting us to deep, time-consuming, manual validation. In fact, we have directly discussed this limitation with the developers of major SBOM tools. They confirmed that the absence of function-level metadata in mainstream vulnerability advisories (e.g. NVD, GHSA) is a known, critical bottleneck, but extracting this information at scale remains an unsolved industry challenge. This consensus further justifies our deep manual validation as the only rigorous approach currently available.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Based on this limitation, we call for a community effort to improve mainstream vulnerability advisories by including standardized, machine-readable <span id="S6.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">affected_symbols</span> in structured form, rather than only in narrative text, which would significantly improve the precision and scalability of reachability-aware analysis and make vulnerability reports more actionable in practice.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">6.5. </span>A Practical Pipeline for Actionable Vulnerability Reporting

<div id="S6.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Based on our findings, we outline a practical, two-stage pipeline designed to guide practitioners from the current state of noisy, package-level alerts to more actionable, context-aware vulnerability reports. This pipeline defines clear responsibilities for both software developers and security tool builders <span id="footnote5" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>5</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>5</sup> <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">5</span> We have contacted the developers of both <span id="footnote5.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> and <span id="footnote5.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Grype</span> regarding these issues, and are currently in discussions with them.</span></span></span>.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.5.1. </span>Stage 1: Ground-Truth SBOM Generation via Lock Files

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The foundational stage of this pipeline is to ensure the integrity of the input: the SBOM itself. Our research confirms that inconsistencies arise when SBOM generators attempt to resolve dependencies from project files. To build a source of truth, this stage requires two actions:

</div>

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S6.SS5.SSS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">For Developers:</span> Standardize on using strong PMs that generate lock files. These lock files must be committed to version control as the definitive record of all direct and transitive dependencies for a reproducible build.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S6.SS5.SSS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">For SBOM Tool Builders:</span> Treat lock files as the only authoritative source. Tools should parse lock files directly, rather than implementing complex and error-prone dependency resolution logic. This guarantees the generated SBOM is a high-fidelity representation of what is actually used in production.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">6.5.2. </span>Stage 2: Finer Granularity Analysis via Reachability

<div id="S6.SS5.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S6.SS5.SSS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">For Security Scanners:</span> The next generation of vulnerability scanners must move beyond simple version matching and incorporate reachability analysis. As validated by our function call experiment, this layer of analysis filters out the vast majority of non-exploitable vulnerabilities by determining if the vulnerable code is actually invoked. This is the key to transforming noisy alert streams into actionable intelligence and directly combating developer alert fatigue.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">7. </span>Conclusions and Future Work

<div id="S7.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this study, we first address the foundational challenge of SBOM accuracy. Through a large-scale evaluation of 2,414 open-source projects, we demonstrated that utilizing lock files as the primary input enables popular SBOM generators like <span id="S7.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Syft</span> and <span id="S7.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Trivy</span> to produce consistent, complete, and reliable SBOMs. This establishes a crucial first step for any practical SBOM-based SSC pipeline.

</div>

<div id="S7.p2" class="ltx_para">

However, our study reveals that even these accurate SBOMs are not a silver bullet for vulnerability management. Our manual evaluation of downstream scanners uncovered a more profound flaw in the current ecosystem: an overwhelming 92.0% false positive rate in our sampled dataset. This finding demonstrates that the current generation of SBOM-based scanners is fundamentally inadequate for practical use, burying developers in unactionable alerts and undermining the trustworthiness of the entire process.

</div>

<div id="S7.p3" class="ltx_para">

This limitation arises from the coarse granularity of information typically provided by SBOMs, which operate at the package level. A package may include a large number of functions, and the presence of a single vulnerable function can result in the entire package—and by extension, the software—being flagged as vulnerable, even if the function is never invoked. For practitioners, this overgeneralization directly leads to wasted resources on manual triage and encourages ignoring security alerts, which ultimately degrades the security posture.

</div>

<div id="S7.p4" class="ltx_para">

Our work not only identifies this critical challenge but also validates a clear path forward. Our large-scale, multi-language study provides the first comprehensive, empirical evidence that reachability analysis is a fundamental requirement across the board. Our function call analysis serves as a generalizable proof-of-concept, demonstrating that reachability analysis can successfully prune a majority of these false positives. Our future work will build upon our validated approach by exploring more advanced static or dynamic analyses to handle complex cases. Solving this issue is paramount to unlocking the full potential of SBOMs and finally delivering on their promise of precise and actionable security insights for practitioners.

</div>

</div>

<td id="A1.T9.2.1.14.4" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="A1.T9.2.1.14.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">121</span></td>

<td id="A1.T9.2.1.14.6" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb ltx_border_t"><span id="A1.T9.2.1.14.6.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">74.4% (Avg)</span></td>

</div>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 9. </span>Complete evaluation results of automated vulnerability pruning on 12 Go repositories with repository sources. (<span class="math inline"><em>V</em><sub><em>r</em></sub></span>: Reported, <span class="math inline"><em>V</em><sub><em>c</em></sub></span>: Confirmed)</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this appendix, we provide the comprehensive data obtained from our automated reachability analysis on the Go ecosystem, as discussed in Section <a href="#S5.SS7" class="ltx_ref" title="5.7. Feasibility of Automated Reachability Analysis ‣ 5. Evaluation ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5.7</span></a>. We selected 12 prominent, open-source Go repositories covering various domains, including web servers, databases, and CLI tools. To simulate a realistic scenario of software maintenance where vulnerabilities may have accumulated, we targeted historical release versions (the first released version in 2025) rather than the latest <span id="A1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">HEAD</span> commits.

</div>

<div id="A1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#A1.T9" class="ltx_ref" title="Table 9 ‣ Appendix A Detailed Results of Automated Go Analysis ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a> details the specific versions analyzed and the comparison results. The column $V_{r}$ (Reported) represents the number of vulnerabilities flagged by standard SBOM-based scanners based solely on package version matching. The column $V_{c}$ represents the number of vulnerabilities where the specific <span id="A1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">affected_symbols</span> (as defined in the Go Vulnerability Database) were confirmed to be present in the compiled binary’s symbol table by our automated tool. The <span id="A1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Reduction Rate</span> is calculated as ${({V_{r} - V_{c}})}/V_{r}$, indicating the percentage of false positives pruned by our reachability analysis.

</div>

<div id="A1.p3" class="ltx_para">

The results demonstrate a consistent pattern across different types of applications: the majority of dependencies flagged as vulnerable are dead code that is eliminated during the compilation process or never invoked by the application logic. The significant reduction in false positives (74.4% on average) empirically validates that metadata-driven reachability analysis is a highly effective strategy for improving the signal-to-noise ratio in vulnerability reporting.

</div>

<div id="A1.p4" class="ltx_para">

Furthermore, to demonstrate the proactive utility of our approach on actively developed software, we also applied our automated analysis to the latest <span id="A1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">HEAD</span> commits of these same 12 repositories. This scan identified a new, reachable vulnerability in one of the projects. Following ethical security research practices, we performed a responsible disclosure by privately reporting our findings to the project’s maintainers. The vulnerability was subsequently acknowledged and patched by the developers. This real-world validation not only confirms the effectiveness of our metadata-driven reachability analysis but also underscores its value as a practical tool for developers to proactively secure their software, moving beyond simply triaging scanner alerts.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

id="A2.F3.pic1" class="ltx_picture ltx_centering" /></span>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3. </span>Distribution of detected vulnerabilities per repository across different programming languages, comparing Trivy and Grype scanners.<span id="acmlabel3" class="ltx_acm_description ltx_centering ltx_nodisplay">Distribution of detected vulnerabilities per repository across different programming languages, comparing Trivy and Grype scanners.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para">

As illustrated in Figure <a href="#acmlabel3" class="ltx_ref" title="Figure 3 ‣ Appendix B Vulnerability Distribution ‣ A Reality Check on SBOM-based Vulnerability Management: An Empirical Study and A Path Forward"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, the volume of reported vulnerabilities varies significantly depending on the language ecosystem. Ruby repositories exhibit the highest median number of vulnerabilities and the widest variance, indicating a potentially more complex dependency chain or a higher historical vulnerability density within its ecosystem. Conversely, PHP repositories demonstrate the lowest overall vulnerability counts. However, reporting a higher raw count of vulnerabilities does not necessarily equate to better security posture. An inflated detection rate often correlates with a higher FPR, which increases alert fatigue for developers.

</div>

Our team has already identified <a href="https://github.com/arXiv/html_feedback/issues" class="ltx_ref" target="_blank">the following issues</a>. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for all.

Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a <a href="https://github.com/brucemiller/LaTeXML/wiki/Porting-LaTeX-packages-for-LaTeXML" class="ltx_ref" target="_blank">list of packages that need conversion</a>, and welcome <a href="https://github.com/brucemiller/LaTeXML/issues" class="ltx_ref" target="_blank">developer contributions</a>.
